Shuzhen Lv
a

Abstract




Joint equidistributions of mesh patterns 123 and 132 with antipodal shadings

Abstract.

The study of joint equidistributions of mesh patterns 123 and 132 with the same symmetric shadings was recently initiated by Kitaev and Lv, where 75 of 80 potential joint equidistributions were proven. In this paper, we prove 112 out of 126 potential joint equidistributions of mesh patterns 123 and 132 with the same antipodal shadings. As a byproduct, we present 562 joint equidistribution results for non-symmetric and non-antipodal shadings. To achieve this, we construct bijections, find recurrence relations, and obtain generating functions. Moreover, we demonstrate that the joint distributions of several pairs of mesh patterns are related to the unsigned Stirling numbers of the first kind.

Keywords:
mesh pattern; joint equidistribution; antipodal shading; Stirling number of the first kind

AMS Subject Classifications:
05A05, 05A15.

1
Introduction

An
n
-permutation is a bijection of the set
[n]:=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.
We denote
S_{n}
the set of all
n
-permutations.
Patterns in permutations have garnered significant attention in the literature (see
[
6
]
), with numerous researchers actively exploring this area. The concept of a
mesh pattern
was introduced by Brändén and Claesson
[
4
]
. A
mesh pattern
of length
k
is defined as a pair
(\tau,R)
, where
\tau\in S_{k}
and
R
is a subset of
[0,k]\times[0,k]
, with
[0,k]
denoting the set of integers from 0 to
k
.
Let
({i,j})
denote the box whose corners have coordinates
(i,j)
,
(i,j+1)
,
(i+1,j+1)
, and
(i+1,j)
. The horizontal lines (resp., vertical lines) represent the values (resp., positions) in the pattern. Mesh patterns are formed by shading boxes in
R
. The figure

represents the mesh pattern with
\tau=132
and
R=\{({0,0}),({1,1}),({1,2}),({3,1})\}
. Many papers have focused on the investigation of mesh patterns, see
[
2
,
5
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
]
.

The
reverse, complement, and inverse
of
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
, are respectively defined as
\pi^{r}=\pi_{n}\pi_{n-1}\cdots\pi_{1}
,
\pi^{c}=(n+1-\pi_{1})(n+1-\pi_{2})\cdots(n+1-\pi_{n})
, and
\pi^{i}=\pi^{i}_{1}\pi^{i}_{2}\cdots\pi^{i}_{n}
such that
\pi^{i}_{j}=\pi_{m}
if
\pi_{j}=m
, for any
1\leq j,m\leq n
. Given a mesh pattern
(\tau,R)
, the corresponding operations are defined as

\displaystyle(\tau,R)^{r}

\displaystyle=(\tau^{r},R^{r}),\text{ where }R^{r}=\{(n-x,y):(x,y)\in R\};

\displaystyle(\tau,R)^{c}

\displaystyle=(\tau^{c},R^{c}),\text{ where }R^{c}=\{(x,n-y):(x,y)\in R\};

\displaystyle(\tau,R)^{i}

\displaystyle=(\tau^{r},R^{i}),\text{ where }R^{i}=\{(y,x):(x,y)\in R\}.

For instance, for the mesh pattern
p=(213,\{(0,1),(1,3),(2,2)\})
, the specific operations are applied as follows.

A sequence
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{i_{1}}\pi_{i_{2}}\cdots\pi_{i_{k}}
is an
occurrence
of a mesh pattern
p=(\tau,R)
in a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}\cdots\pi_{n}
if
(i)

\pi^{\prime}
is order-isomorphic to
\tau
, and
(ii)
the shaded squares given by
R
do not contain any elements of
\pi
not appearing in
\pi^{\prime}
. Otherwise, we say that
\pi

avoids

p
, and we let
S_{n}(p)
denote the set of such permutations.
For instance, the permutation 24513 contains 2 occurrences of the mesh pattern
(132,\{({0,0}),({1,1}),({1,2}),({3,1})\})
as the subsequence 241 and 243.

Two patterns
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are said to be
Wilf-equivalent
, denoted
q_{1}\sim q_{2}
, if
|S_{n}(q_{1})|=|S_{n}(q_{2})|
. Moreover,
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
are called
equidistributed
, denoted
q_{1}\sim_{d}q_{2}
, if for any nonnegative integers
n
and
k
, the number of
n
-permutations with
k
occurrences of
q_{1}
is equal to that with
k
occurrences of
q_{2}
. A stronger notion,
joint equidistribution
of two mesh patterns, denoted
(q_{1},q_{2})\sim_{d}(q_{2},q_{1})
or
q_{1}\sim_{jd}q_{2}
, means that the number of
n
-permutations with
k
occurrences of
q_{1}
and
\ell
occurrences of
q_{2}
is equal to that with
\ell
occurrences of
q_{1}
and
k
occurrences of
q_{2}
for any nonnegative integers
n
,
k
, and
\ell
.

Patterns 123 and 132 remain invariant under both the group-theoretic inverse and the reflection of the elements across the diagonal. This motivates the authors of
[
10
]
to study the joint equidistributions of mesh patterns 123 and 132 with certain shadings. We say that a mesh pattern has an
antipodal shading
if, for any
i\neq j
, precisely one of
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
is shaded. The notion of an antipodal shading of a mesh pattern was first introduced in the context of multidimensional permutations in
[
1
]
. More relevant to our paper, Kitaev and Lv
[
10
]
investigated the joint equidistributions of the mesh patterns 123 and 132 with the same symmetric shadings. Also, Lv and Zhang
[
11
]
extended the study to the joint equidistributions of the mesh patterns 123 and 321 with the same symmetric and antipodal shadings.

In this paper, we establish 112 joint equidistributions for mesh patterns 123 and 132 with antipodal shadings. Table
1
presents a summary of our results, which are classified into 8 disjoint classes of shadings considered in Sections
2
–
5
. An additional 4 pairs with other antipodal shadings, considered in Section
6
, can be found in Table
10
. Finally, in Section
7
, we give 14 conjectured joint equidistributions as presented in Table
11
, and 562 relevant joint equidistributions, which are given by non-antipodal and non-symmetric shadings for the mesh patterns, as listed in Table
12
.

To illustrate the information presented in Table
1
, consider the pair of mesh patterns

. In general, throughout the paper, we use
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
to represent mesh patterns 123 and 132, respectively, with the same shadings depending on the context. Moreover, we always let
p_{1}
(resp.,
p_{2}
) be the length-2 mesh pattern obtained from
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) by removing the bottom row and leftmost column, as in the example above. Also, by abusing the notation, all of our bijective maps used to prove joint equidistributions in this paper are denoted by
f
. Finally, for a pair of mesh patterns
\{q_{1},q_{2}\}
, we often omit the brackets and the comma.

For the patterns in Sections
2
–
5
, we note that only the results for type
X^{(i)}
shadings, for
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, require justifications, since all other results follow from the fact that type
Y^{(i)}
shading is obtained from type
X^{(i)}
shading by applying the inverse operation. Hence, we omit type
Y^{(i)}
shadings from our considerations. Also, in this paper, we number pairs of mesh patterns 123 and 132 with the same type
X^{(i)}
(resp.,
Y^{(i)}
) shading by
X^{(i)}_{j}
(resp.,
Y^{(i)}_{j}
) for
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
j\geq 1
, and as noted above, we only provide proofs for the mesh patterns with type
X^{(i)}_{j}
shadings.

Let
\mathcal{T}(n,k,\ell)
be the set of
n
-permutations with
k
occurrences of
q_{1}
and
\ell
occurrences of
q_{2}
in questions. We define
T_{n,k,\ell}=|\mathcal{T}(n,k,\ell)|
and the generating function

T_{n}(x,y)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}T_{n,k,\ell}\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}.

Analogously, define the set
\mathcal{H}(n,k,\ell)
,
H_{n,k,\ell}=|\mathcal{H}(n,k,\ell)|
, and

H_{n}(x,y)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}H_{n,k,\ell}\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell},

for the pair
\{p_{1},\hskip 2.84544ptp_{2}\}
.

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
. The following result allows us to reduce considerations to length-2 mesh patterns in this case.

Lemma 2.1
.

For type
X^{(1)}
patterns, we have
q_{1}\sim_{jd}q_{2}
if and only if
p_{1}\sim_{jd}p_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\pi=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
,
A=\{\pi_{i}:\pi_{i}>\pi_{1},2\leq i\leq n\}
and
B=\{\pi_{i}:\pi_{i}<\pi_{1},2\leq i\leq n\}
. Each occurrence of
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
), if any, must begin with
\pi_{1}
, and
p_{1}
(resp.,
p_{2}
) will involve the elements in
A
, and no elements in
B
. But then it is clear that the joint equidistribution of
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
is equivalent to the joint equidistribution of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, which completes our proof.
∎

2.1
Cases proved via complementation or reversal

This subsection is about the 32 pairs presented in Table
2
. Each pair
\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
associated with the mesh pattern
X^{(1)}_{j}
, where
j\in\{1,\ldots,16\}
, exhibits symmetry either horizontally or vertically. Therefore, the map
f
that gives the joint equidistributions can be constructed by applying complement or reverse operations. Moreover, the same joint distribution of the pairs in each row of Table
2
follows from the complement, reverse, and inverse operations. Here, we demonstrate the operations for the pairs
\{X^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4}
as an example, and the remaining three sets of pairs of mesh patterns are treated in the same manner:

. Hence, by Lemma

, the mesh patterns in the pairs
X_{17}^{(1)}
and
X_{18}^{(1)}
are jointly equidistributed, and the pairs have the same joint distribution, which completes our proof. ∎

The computer also suggests that pairs
\{X^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=9}^{18}
and
\{Y^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=9}^{18}
have the same joint distribution. Theorem
2.4
below will prove this fact by deriving recurrence relations and generating functions for the joint distributions.

Recall that the
unsigned Stirling number of the first kind
(
[
12
, A132393]
) is defined by
c(n,k)=0
if
n<k
or
k=0
, except
c(0,0)=1
, and

c(n,k)=(n-1)c(n-1,k)+c(n-1,k-1).

Theorem 2.4
.

The pairs
\{X^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=9}^{18}
and
\{Y^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=9}^{18}
have the same joint distribution.
Moreover, all pairs satisfy

\displaystyle T_{n,k,\ell}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(n-1)!\,\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}\,\frac{1}{i!}\,c(i-1,k+\ell),&\text{}k\neq 0\text{ or }l\neq 0,\\[6.0pt]
2(n-1)!,&\text{}k=\ell=0.\end{array}\right.

Equivalently,

\displaystyle T_{n}(x,y)=(n-1)!\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}\,\frac{1}{i!}\,\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,c(i-1,k+\ell)\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}+2(n-1)!.

(3)

By Subsection
2.1
and Theorem
2.3
, it suffices to verify that the pairs
X^{(1)}_{9}
,
X^{(1)}_{13}
and
X^{(1)}_{17}
satisfy (
2.4
) and (
3
). To achieve this, we will consider length-2 pairs corresponding to these three pairs in the next lemma.

Lemma 2.5
.

The pairs

\{\hskip-2.84544pt\raisebox{2.58334pt}{
\hbox to15.24pt{\vbox to15.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.15024pt\lower-0.15024pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 6.89 L 6.89 6.89 L 6.89 0 Z M 6.89 6.89}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 6.89 M 0 6.89 L 0 13.78 L 6.89 13.78 L 6.89 6.89 Z M 6.89 13.78}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.78 0 M 13.78 0 L 13.78 6.89 L 20.67 6.89 L 20.67 0 Z M 20.67 6.89}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.78 6.89 M 13.78 6.89 L 13.78 13.78 L 20.67 13.78 L 20.67 6.89 Z M 20.67 13.78}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}{}{
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{
}{

satisfy

\displaystyle H_{n,k,\ell}=\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,c(n-1,k+\ell).

(4)

Equivalently,

\displaystyle H_{n}(x,y)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,c(n-1,k+\ell)\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}.

(5)

Proof.

(i)

Consider the pair
\{p_{1}\hskip-2.84544pt=\hskip-2.84544pt\raisebox{2.58334pt}{
\hbox to15.24pt{\vbox to15.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.15024pt\lower-0.15024pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 6.89 L 6.89 6.89 L 6.89 0 Z M 6.89 6.89}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 6.89 M 0 6.89 L 0 13.78 L 6.89 13.78 L 6.89 6.89 Z M 6.89 13.78}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.78 0 M 13.78 0 L 13.78 6.89 L 20.67 6.89 L 20.67 0 Z M 20.67 6.89}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.78 6.89 M 13.78 6.89 L 13.78 13.78 L 20.67 13.78 L 20.67 6.89 Z M 20.67 13.78}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}{}{
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{
}{
}{
}{
}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.07 0.07 M 0.07 6.89 L 20.6 6.89 M 0.07 13.78 L 20.6 13.78 M 6.89 0.07 L 6.89 20.6 M 13.78 0.07 L 13.78 20.6 M 20.6 20.6}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {{

. We obtain each
n
-permutation
\pi=\pi\cdots\pi_{n}
counted by the left-hand side of the equation (
5
) by inserting the largest element
n
into an
(n-1)
-permutation
\pi^{\prime}
. Specifically, inserting
n
immediately before the first entry creates an additional occurrence of
p_{2}
, in the form of
n\pi_{n}
, which contributes to
y\,H_{n-1}(x,y)
. Conversely, inserting
n
immediately after the last entry creates an extra occurrence of
p_{1}
with
\pi_{1}n
, which is counted by
x\,H_{n-1}(x,y)
. In any other slot, the insertion preserves the number of occurrences of both patterns, thereby contributing to
(n-2)H_{n-1}(x,y)
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle H_{n}(x,y)=(n+x+y-2)\,H_{n-1}(x,y).

(6)

It is easy to see that the initial conditions in (
6
) are the same as those in (
5
). Moreover, we prove that (
5
) satisfies (
6
) as follows.

\displaystyle(n+x+y-2)\,H_{n-1}(x,y)=

\displaystyle(n+x+y-2)\,\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,c(n-2,k+\ell)\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}(n-2)\,\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,c(n-2,k+\ell)\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-2}\,\binom{k+\ell-1}{k-1}\,c(n-2,k+\ell-1)\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}\,\binom{k+\ell-1}{k}\,c(n-2,k+\ell-1)\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\Big(\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,(n-2)\,c(n-2,k+\ell)+\left(\binom{k+\ell-1}{k-1}+\binom{k+\ell-1}{k}\right)c(n-2,k+\ell-1)\Big)\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,((n-2)\,c(n-2,k+\ell)+c(n-2,k+\ell-1))\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,c(n-1,k+\ell)\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}=H_{n}(x,y).

By extracting the coefficients from both sides of (
6
), we can directly obtain (
4
).

,
observe that occurrences of both patterns in a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{n}
begin with
\pi_{1}
. Define

C=\{\pi_{j}:\pi_{j}<\pi_{1}\}
and
D=\{\pi_{j}:\pi_{j}>\pi_{1}\}
, and suppose there are
i
elements in
C
.
For each permutation counted by the left-hand side of (
4
), the permutation formed by
C
contains
\ell
right-to-left maxima, that is,
\ell
occurrences of the mesh pattern

, which is given by the unsigned Stirling numbers of the first kind,
c(i,\ell)
, as found in
[
13
, Corollary 1.3.11]
and mentioned in
[
1
, Case 1]
. Moreover, the permutation formed by
D
contains
k
occurrences of the pattern

, contributing a factor of
c(n-i-1,k)
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle H_{n,k,\ell}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{i}c(i,\ell)\,c(n-i-1,k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,c(n-1,k+\ell),

where the last line follows from a variation of the Chu-Vandermonde identity, see
[
3
,
14
, Equation (17)]
, also see
[
11
, Equation (5)]
. Furthermore, (
5
) follows from (
4
).

{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.78 6.89 M 15.23 6.89 C 15.23 7.69 14.58 8.34 13.78 8.34 C 12.98 8.34 12.33 7.69 12.33 6.89 C 12.33 6.09 12.98 5.44 13.78 5.44 C 14.58 5.44 15.23 6.09 15.23 6.89 Z M 13.78 6.89}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\}
, our arguments for all the terms in (
5
) are the same as those in
(i)
, except for the term
y\,H_{n-1}(x,y)
, which is replaced by the corresponding insertion immediately before the last entry, thereby creating an extra occurrence of
p_{2}
.

Thus, the proof is complete.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

For each permutation counted by the left-hand side of (
3
), suppose there are
i
elements in
A
. For
i=0
or
1
, the permutation formed by the remaining
n-1
or
n-2
elements in
B
can be any permutation. For
i\geq 2
, the permutation formed by
A
is counted by
H_{i}(x,y)
, and the permutation formed by
B
can be any permutation. Thus, we have

\displaystyle T_{n}(x,y)=

\displaystyle 2(n-1)!+\,\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}\,\binom{n-1}{i}\,(n-i-1)!\,H_{i}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}\,\binom{n-1}{i}\,(n-i-1)!\,\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,c(i-1,k+\ell)\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}+2(n-1)!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n-1)!\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{n-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\,\frac{1}{i!}\,\binom{k+\ell}{k}\,c(i-1,k+\ell)\,x^{k}\,y^{\ell}+2(n-1)!.

By extracting the coefficients from both sides of (
3
), we can directly obtain (
2.4
).
∎

The computer also suggests that the mesh patterns of pairs
\{X^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{18}
and
\{Y^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{18}
are Wilf-equivalent. We will proof this fact in the following corollary.

Corollary 2.6
.

The pairs
\{X^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{18}
and
\{Y^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{18}
are Wilf-equivalent.

(
X^{(1)}_{5}
) satisfy
T_{n,0,0}=2(n-1)!
.

For the pair
X^{(1)}_{1}
, consider any
n
-permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{n}
. Let
A=\{\pi_{i}:\pi_{i}>\pi_{1},2\leq i\leq n\}
and
B=\{\pi_{i}:\pi_{i}<\pi_{1},2\leq i\leq n\}
. The permutation
\pi
counted by
T_{n,0,0}
can be obtained from the following two cases:

•

If
A=\emptyset
, then the remaining
n-1
elements involved in
B
can form any permutation, resulting in
(n-1)!
permutations.

•

If
A\neq\emptyset
, then
i=1
. Otherwise, there would be at least one occurrence of

or

in
\pi
. The remaining elements in
B
have no restrictions. Thus, this case gives
(n-1)!
permutations.

By summing over the two cases above, we obtain
T_{n,0,0}=2(n-1)!
. The same approach can be applied to the pair
X^{(1)}_{5}
, thus, we omit the details here. The proof is complete.
∎

(Pairs
\{X^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=19}^{22}
and
\{Y^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=19}^{22}
)
.

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\}
associated with the pair
X^{(1)}_{19}
.

Observe that any occurrence of
p_{1}
or
p_{2}
in a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{n}\in S_{n}
must involve two consecutive elements in
\pi
. Additionally,
\pi_{i}\pi_{i+1}
is an occurrence of
p_{1}
(resp.,
p_{2}
) if and only if
\pi_{i}
(resp.,
\pi_{i+1}
) is a left-to-right minimum and
\pi_{i+1}=n
(resp.,
\pi_{i}=n
). Thus, either pattern appears at most once in a permutation. We let

f(\pi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\pi,&\text{if }\pi\in\mathcal{H}(n,0,0)\cup\mathcal{H}(n,1,1),\\[6.0pt]
\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{i-1}\pi_{i+1}\pi_{i}\pi_{i+2}\cdots\pi_{n},&\text{if }\pi\in\mathcal{H}(n,1,0)\cup\mathcal{H}(n,0,1).\end{array}\right.

Clearly,
f
is a bijection that maps
\mathcal{H}(n,k,\ell)
to
\mathcal{H}(n,k,\ell)
, proving the joint equidistribution for the pair
\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
, and thereby for the pair
X^{(1)}_{19}
.
The proof is complete.
∎

For the patterns of type
X^{(2)}
,
let
\pi\in S_{n}
, and
x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots>x_{t}
be the sequence of left-to-right minima in
\pi
. Observe that any occurrence of each pair of these patterns begin with an
x_{i}
. Moreover, referring to Figure
1
, such an occurrence has the pattern
p
appearing in
A_{i}
, which means occurrences of mesh patterns of type
X^{(2)}
must be entirely within
\{x_{i}\}\cup A_{i}
for some
i
.

3.1

Observe that the shadings of the patterns of the pairs
\{p_{1},\hskip 2.84544ptp_{2}\}
associated with the pairs
\{X^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{8}
exhibit horizontal symmetry.
We establish a bijective map
f
as follows: for a given
\pi
, apply the complement operation on each
A_{i}
in
\pi
, that is, inside each
A_{i}
replace its largest element by its smallest element (in the same position), its next largest element by next smallest element (in the same position), and so on. Clearly, as shown in Figure
1
, occurrences of the patterns in any pair
\{X^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{8}
will be swapped and no new occurrences of the patterns from the same pair are introduced. This proves the desired joint equidistributions.

.

Proof.

For
X^{(2)}_{9}
,
x_{i}ab
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) in
\pi\in S_{n}
, where
1\leq i\leq t
, if and only if
a=x_{i}+1
(resp.,
b=x_{i}+1
), and
b
(
a
) is immediately to the right (resp., left) of
a
(
b
) with
b>a
(resp.,
a>b
) in
A_{i}
. Therefore, each
A_{i}
contains at most one occurrence of

p_{1}
and one occurrence of
p_{2}
. We construct the map
f
as follows:

(i)

If
A_{i}\in\mathcal{H}(n_{i},0,0)\cup\mathcal{H}(n_{i},1,1)
, then
A_{i}
remains unchanged.

(ii)

If
A_{i}\in\mathcal{H}(n_{i},1,0)\cup\mathcal{H}(n_{i},0,1)
, then we swap the elements
a
and
b
,

where
n_{i}=|A_{i}|
and
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,t\}
. Clearly,
f
is a bijection, and the theorem is proved.
∎

and

.

Proof.

For
X^{(2)}_{10}
,
x_{i}ab
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) in
\pi\in S_{n}
, where
1\leq i\leq t
, if and only if
b
(resp.,
a
) is the largest element to the right of
x_{i}
, and
a
(resp.,
b
) is immediately to the left (resp., right) of
b
(resp.,
a
) with
a<b
(resp.,
b<a
) in
A_{i}
. We claim that each permutation contains at most one occurrence of
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
). Otherwise, suppose
x_{j}a^{\prime}b^{\prime}
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
that differs from
x_{i}ab
(the case of
q_{2}
can be considered in a similar way).

•

If
x_{i}=x_{j}
, then
b^{\prime}=b
, which also leads to
a^{\prime}=a
, a contradiction.

•

If
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
, then assume, w.l.o.g., that
x_{i}>x_{j}
. We see that
x_{j}
is positioned between
x_{i}
and
b
because of the shaded area
(3,0)
. It must be the case that
b^{\prime}=b
, which also leads to a contradiction due to the element
x_{i}
.

The claim is proved. Next, we construct the map
f
as follows:

(i)

If
\pi\in\mathcal{T}(n,0,0)\cup\mathcal{T}(n,1,1)
, then
f(\pi)=\pi
.

(ii)

If
\pi\in\mathcal{T}(n,1,0)\cup\mathcal{T}(n,0,1)
, then we swap the elements
a
and
b
,

where
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,t\}
. Clearly,
f
is a bijection, and the theorem is proved.
∎

(Pairs
X^{(2)}_{11}
and
Y^{(2)}_{11}

.

Proof.

For
X^{(2)}_{11}
,
x_{i}ab
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) in
\pi\in S_{n}
, where
1\leq i\leq t
, if and only if
a
is a left-to-right minimum in
A_{i}
, and
b=a+1
(resp.,
b=a-1
) is a right-to-left minimum. Therefore, each
A_{i}
contains at most one occurrence of
p_{1}
and one occurrence of
p_{2}
, which allows us to use the same map
f
as in Theorem
3.1
.
∎

Proof.

For
X^{(2)}_{12}
,
x_{i}ab
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) in
\pi\in S_{n}
, where
1\leq i\leq t
, if and only if
a
is a left-to-right maximum in
A_{i}
, and
b=a+1
(resp.,
b=a-1
) is a right-to-left maximum. Thus, we can use the same map
f
as in Theorem
3.1
.
∎

For
X^{(2)}_{13}
, we first provide a bijective proof of the joint equidistribution of the pair
\{p_{1}=\raisebox{2.58334pt}{
\hbox to15.24pt{\vbox to15.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.15024pt\lower-0.15024pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 6.89 M 0 6.89 L 0 13.78 L 6.89 13.78 L 6.89 6.89 Z M 6.89 13.78}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 13.78 M 0 13.78 L 0 20.67 L 6.89 20.67 L 6.89 13.78 Z M 6.89 20.67}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.89 6.89 M 6.89 6.89 L 6.89 13.78 L 13.78 13.78 L 13.78 6.89 Z M 13.78 13.78}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.89 13.78 M 6.89 13.78 L 6.89 20.67 L 13.78 20.67 L 13.78 13.78 Z M 13.78 20.67}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{

.

Proof.

Our arguments here are analogous to those in
[
7
, Theorem. 2.3]
, but the swapping of elements is reordered from “right to left” to “top to bottom”. The
details are as follows.

Let
\pi^{(1)}\in S_{n}
and
\pi^{(1)}_{i_{1}}\pi^{(1)}_{i_{2}}\ldots\pi^{(1)}_{i_{t}}
be the subsequence of
\pi^{(1)}
that includes all elements in all occurrences of the patterns. Consider the unique occurrence
t\pi^{(1)}_{i_{j_{1}}}
(or
\pi^{(1)}_{i_{j_{1}}}t
), of a pattern, where
\pi^{(1)}_{i_{j_{1}}}
is the largest element that is involved with the largest element
t
in an occurrence and swap
t
and
\pi^{(1)}_{i_{j_{1}}}
in
\pi
. Such a swap will change exactly one occurrence of a pattern to an occurrence of the other pattern, without introducing or removing any other occurrences of the patterns. Next, consider
\pi^{(2)}=\pi^{(2)}_{1}\ldots\pi^{(2)}_{n}
obtained from
\pi
by swapping
t
and
\pi^{(1)}_{i_{j_{1}}}
.
Suppose
\pi_{i_{j_{2}}}^{(2)}\pi_{i_{j_{3}}}^{(2)}
or
\pi_{i_{j_{3}}}^{(2)}\pi_{i_{j_{2}}}^{(2)}
is the unique occurrence of either pattern,
where
\pi_{i_{j_{2}}}^{(2)}
is the largest element in all occurrences within
\pi^{(2)}
, and
\pi_{i_{j_{3}}}^{(2)}
is the largest element that,
together with
\pi_{i_{j_{2}}}^{(2)}
, forms an occurrence, except for the previously considered case
\pi^{(1)}_{i_{j_{1}}}t
(or
t\pi^{(1)}_{i_{j_{1}}}
).
Swap
\pi^{(2)}_{i_{j_{2}}}
and
\pi^{(2)}_{i_{j_{3}}}
and we obtain
\pi^{(3)}
. And so forth. Eventually, all occurrences of the patterns in
\pi
will be swapped without affecting other elements in permutation
\pi
, which proves their joint equidistribution.

Figure 2:
Permutations
\pi^{(1)}=263518497
(to the left) and
\pi^{\prime}=325916487
(to the right) illustrating the proof of Lemma
3.5
. The circled dots represent the left-to-right maxima in
\pi^{(1)}
, and the white dots represent the second elements of occurrences of
p_{2}
.

For example, for the permutation
\pi
in Figure
2
, we do the procedure as follows:
\pi^{(1)}=26351\uline{8}4\uline{9}7\xrightarrow{\text{swap 8 \& 9}}2\uline{6}351\uline{9}487\xrightarrow{\text{swap 6 \& 9}}2\uline{9}3\uline{5}16487\xrightarrow{\text{swap 9 \& 5}}2\uline{53}916487\xrightarrow{\text{swap 5 \& 3}}\uline{2}\uline{3}5916487\xrightarrow{\text{swap 2 \& 3}}325916487
, so that
\pi^{\prime}=325916487
.
Observe that the occurrences 26, 68 and 89 of
p_{1}
in
\pi^{(1)}
are replaced, respectively, by the occurrence 32, 96 and 98 of
p_{2}
in
\pi^{\prime}
, while the occurrences 63 and 65 of
p_{2}
in
\pi^{(1)}
are replaced by the occurrences 35 and 59 of
p_{1}
in
\pi^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof.

For
X^{(2)}_{13}
, note that the rightmost element in
A_{i+1}
must be to the left of the leftmost element in
A_{i}
, as depicted in Figure
3
. Therefore, the applications of the process described in Lemma
3.5
in each
A_{i}
do not affect each other.
∎

A_{i}
A_{i+1}
x_{i}
x_{i+1}
\ldots
\ldots

Figure 3:
The structure of permutations containing occurrences of patterns in the proof of Theorem
3.6
.

For each pair
\{X^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=14}^{17}
, similarly to the proof of Theorem
3.2
, observe that there is at most one occurrence of either pattern in a permutation. This observation allows us to use the bijection
f
described in the proof of Theorem
3.2
and provides the proofs of the following theorems, which are omitted.

Remark
.

Note that the proofs of the joint equidistribution results of type
X^{(2)}
in this section are independent of whether the area
(3,0)
is shaded or not. This implies that the joint equistribution also holds for the type
p
,
with
p
replaced by the mesh patterns of
p
that are associated with
X^{(2)}
, and therefore also holds for the type
p
,
with
p
replaced by the mesh patterns
p
associated with
Y^{(2)}
. We state these joint equidistribution results in Table
12
.

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
.

Proof.

Considering the pair
X^{(3)}_{9}
, let
x_{i}\pi_{j}\pi_{t}
be an occurrence of
q_{1}
or
q_{2}
in a permutation
\pi\in S_{n}
, where
1\leq i\leq s
,
2\leq j,t\leq n
. We have
t=j+1
and
\pi_{t}=n
(resp.,
\pi_{j}=n
) for
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
). Therefore, there is at most one occurrence of
p_{1}
or
p_{2}
in
\pi
. Furthermore, w.l.o.g., suppose
x_{\ell}\pi_{j}\pi_{t}
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
or
q_{2}
with
i<\ell\leq s
; then
x_{\ell}
is positioned between
x_{i}
and
\pi_{j}
because of the shaded area
(2,0)
, which contradicts our supposition due to the element
x_{i}
. Thus, each permutation contains at most one occurrence of either pattern in
X^{(3)}_{9}
. Therefore, the map
f
is constructed in the same way as in Theorem
3.2
.∎

.

Proof.

For the pair
X^{(4)}_{1}
, if
x_{i}ab
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) in a permutation, where
1\leq i\leq t
, then
b
(resp.,
a
) is the maximal element following
x_{i}
, and
a
is immediately to the left of
b
(resp.,
b
is immediately to the right of
a
). We claim that each permutation contains at most one occurrence of
q_{1}
or
q_{2}
. Otherwise, suppose
x_{j}a^{\prime}b^{\prime}
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
that differs from
x_{i}ab
(a similar consideration applies for the case of
q_{2}
).

(i)

If
x_{i}=x_{j}
, then
b^{\prime}=b
, which also leads to
a^{\prime}=a
, a contradiction.

(ii)

If
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
, then assume, w.l.o.g.,
i<j\leq t
. We see that
x_{j}
is positioned between
x_{i}
and
a
due to the shaded areas
(2,0)
and
(3,0)
. This will result in
b^{\prime}=b
, and thereby
a^{\prime}=a
, also a contradiction.

We proved our claim. Thus, we can use the same map
f
as described in the proof of Theorem
3.2
to complete our proof.
∎

.

Proof.

For the pair
X^{(4)}_{2}
, similarly to the proof of Theorem
5.1
, there is at most one occurrence of
q_{1}
or
q_{2}
in
\pi
. Suppose
x_{i}ab
and
x_{j}a^{\prime}b^{\prime}
are two different occurrences of
q_{1}
in a permutation
\pi\in S_{n}
. It must be the case that
b^{\prime}=b=\pi_{n}
, which also results in
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
; otherwise,
a^{\prime}=a
. This will also lead to a contradiction because of the element
x_{i}
. Therefore, we also use the same map
f
as in the proof of Theorem
3.2
, which completes our proof.
∎

For each pair
\{X^{(4)}_{i}\}_{i=3}^{5}
, similarly to the proof of Theorem
5.1
, observe that there is at most one occurrence of either pattern in a permutation. This observation allows us to use the bijection
f
described in the proof of Theorem
3.2
and provides the proofs of the following theorems, which are omitted.

Theorem 5.3

(Pairs
X^{(4)}_{3}
and
Y^{(4)}_{3}
)
.

We have

{}{{}}{}

Theorem 5.4

(Pairs
X^{(4)}_{4}
and
X^{(4)}_{4}
)
.

. Moreover, both pairs have the same joint distribution with the pairs
\{X^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=9}^{18}
and
\{Y^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=9}^{18}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.4
, it suffices to prove
X^{(4)}_{6}
satisfies (
2.4
) and (
3
).
Let

T^{(1)}_{n,k,\ell}:=\{\pi\in\mathcal{T}_{n,k,\ell},\text{ }\pi_{n}=1\},

T^{(2)}_{n,k,\ell}:=\{\pi\in\mathcal{T}_{n,k,\ell},\text{ }\pi_{n}\neq 1\}.

It is clear that

\displaystyle T_{n}(x,y)=T^{(1)}_{n}(x,y)+T^{(2)}_{n}(x,y).

(7)

We claim that

\displaystyle T^{(1)}_{n}(x,y)=

\displaystyle(n-1)!,

(8)

\displaystyle T^{(2)}_{n}(x,y)=

\displaystyle(n+x+y-2)T_{n-1}(x,y)+(3-n-x-y)(n-2)!.

(9)

To explain the two equations, we consider generating all
n
-permutations from
(n-1)
-permutations by inserting the largest element
n
. We have:

•

For (
8
), each permutation
\pi
counted by the left-hand side can only be obtained from the case where
\pi_{n}=1
, by inserting
n
in any slot except immediately after the last entry. Therefore, this case is given by
(n-1)T^{(1)}_{n-1}(x,y)
. Since
T^{(1)}_{2}(x,y)=1
, we obtain (
8
).

•

For (
9
), each permutation
\pi
counted by the left-hand side can be derived from the following cases:

(a)

If
\pi_{n}=1
, then inserting
n
immediately after the last entry results in
\pi_{n}\neq 1
. This insertion preserves the number of the occurrence of both patterns, thereby giving
T^{(1)}_{n-1}(x,y)
.

(b)

If
\pi_{n}\neq 1
, then inserting
n
immediately after the last entry leads to an extra occurrence of
q_{1}
, in the form of
\pi_{j}\pi_{n-1}n
, where
\pi_{j}
represents the leftmost element less than
\pi_{n-1}
. Thus, this case gives
xT^{(2)}_{n-1}(x,y)
. Similarly,
yT^{(2)}_{n-1}(x,y)
corresponds to the insertion of
n
immediately before the last entry, which results in an additional occurrence of
q_{1}
formed by
\pi_{j}n\pi_{n}
. In any other slot, the insertion of
n
preserves the number of occurrences of both patterns, contributing to
(n-2)T^{(2)}_{n-1}(x,y)
.

Summing over the above two cases and using (
8
), we obtain (
9
).

Combining (
7
), (
8
), and (
9
), we obtain

\displaystyle T_{n}(x,y)=(n+x+y-2)\,T_{n-1}(x,y)+(2-x-y)\,(n-2)!.

(10)

It is not difficult to verify that (
3
) satisfies (
10
) for any nonnegative integer
n
. Thus, the proof is complete.
∎

.

Proof.

Observe that pairs 113 and 114 can be obtained from each by the inverse operation. Hence, it is sufficient to consider the pair 113. Any occurrence of a pattern in the pair in a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{n}
ends with
\pi_{n}
. Moreover,
\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\pi_{n}
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) if and only if
\pi_{j}=\pi_{n}-1
(resp.,
\pi_{j}=\pi_{n}+1
), and
\pi_{i}
is the largest element to the left of, and smaller than
\pi_{j}
,
i.e.,
\pi_{i}=\max\{\pi_{s}:\pi_{s}<\pi_{j},s<j\}
.

Hence, each permutation contains at most one occurrence of either pattern. The map
f
can then be constructed in the same way as in the proof of Theorem
3.2
to complete our proof. ∎

.

Proof.

Observe that pairs 115 and 116 can be obtained from each by the inverse operation. Hence, it is sufficient to consider the pair 115. Any occurrence of a pattern in the pair in a permutation
\pi=\pi_{1}\cdots\pi_{n}
ends with
\pi_{n}
. Moreover,
\pi_{i}\pi_{j}\pi_{n}
is an occurrence of
q_{1}
(resp.,
q_{2}
) if and only if
\pi_{j}=\pi_{n}-1
(resp.,
\pi_{j}=\pi_{n}+1
), and
\pi_{i}
is the rightmost element to the left of, and smaller than
\pi_{j}
,
i.e.,
i=\max\{s:\pi_{s}<\pi_{j},s<j\}
.

Hence, each permutation contains at most one occurrence of each pattern. The map
f
can be constructed in the same way as in the proof of Theorem
3.2
to complete our proof.
∎

7
Concluding remark

In this paper, we proved 112 joint equidistribution results for mesh patterns with antipodal shadings. We achieve this by constructing bijections, finding recurrence relations, and generating functions. In fact, computer experiments suggest that pairs 113 to 116 have the same joint distribution as pairs
\{X^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=9}^{18}
and
\{Y^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=9}^{18}
, satisfying (
2.4
) and (
3
); we leave justifying this observation as an open problem. Also, Table
11
presents 14 conjectured equidistributions, which we state below as separate conjectures.

Conjecture 4

(Pairs 119 to 126)
.

We have
\raisebox{2.58334pt}{

{{}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 20.67 M 0 20.67 L 0 27.56 L 6.89 27.56 L 6.89 20.67 Z M 6.89 27.56}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

. Moreover, these eight pairs have the same joint distribution as
\{X^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=9}^{18}
and
\{Y^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=9}^{18}
.

Although the subject of our study in this paper is the antipodal shadings of mesh patterns, we conclude by presenting in Table
12
non-antipodal (and non-symmetric) shadings and their types that give joint equidistributions for the mesh patterns 123 and 132. Each of these results can be easily justified through the complement and/or reverse operations, and can essentially be found in one of the Subsections
2.1
,
3.1
, and
4.1
; hence, we omit the proofs.
